\section{Introduction}
The parameters of an effective field theory (EFT), like those of any quantum field theory, depend on the
renormalization scale $\mu$. This dependence is governed by the renormalization-group equations (RGEs) of the theory, which are related
to the additional divergences introduced by the effective operators. Consequently, the RGEs depend on
the field content and on the imposed gauge symmetry, and therefore need to be derived anew for each
individual EFT.

It is however possible to adopt a general framework encompassing an arbitrary number of scalar,
fermion, and vector fields transforming under an arbitrary gauge group, in which the RGEs are
computed once and for all, yielding general template RGEs. Those of a specific EFT then follow by
algebraic and group-theory manipulations, without the need to perform any loop computation:
\begin{equation*}
\boxed{\text{\textbf{\;Gauge group\;}}}\; +  \; \boxed{\text{\textbf{\;Particle content\;}}}\; \underset{\text{Group algebra}}{\longrightarrow}\;  \boxed{\text{\textbf{\;RGEs\;}}}\,.
\end{equation*}

One core premise of EFTs is that they are valid only up to a given cut-off scale $\Lambda$. As a
consequence of the decoupling theorem, the effective couplings are suppressed by inverse powers of
$\Lambda$, which introduces a natural power-counting parameter $f/\Lambda$, where $f$ stands for the
small momenta or mass scales of the EFT. Throughout this chapter, we work up to order $(f/\Lambda)^2$
and retain only terms that are at most $\mathcal{O}(1/\Lambda^2)$, so that double insertions of
dimension-six operators, as well as products of dimension-five and dimension-six Wilson coefficients,
are neglected in the running.

For the dimension-four interactions, several template RGEs already exist in the literature. Some early
work at the two-loop level can be found in
Refs.~\cite{Jack:1984vj,Machacek:1983tz,Machacek:1983fi,Machacek:1984zw}, with corrections later
given in Refs.~\cite{Luo:2002ti,Schienbein:2018fsw}. The two-loop renormalization of vacuum
expectation values in gauge theories was computed in Refs.~\cite{Sperling:2013eva,Sperling:2013xqa}.
The three-loop beta functions for gauge and Yukawa interactions were derived in
Refs.~\cite{Pickering:2001aq,Poole:2019kcm,Mihaila:2012pz} and
Refs.~\cite{Davies:2021mnc,Poole:2019txl}, respectively, while the generic four-loop gauge beta
function is given in Ref.~\cite{Bednyakov:2021qxa}. For models containing scalars and fermions the
three-loop gaugeless RGEs were derived in Refs.~\cite{Jack:2023zjt,Steudtner:2021fzs}, the quartic
scalar coupling was renormalized at three loops in Ref.~\cite{Steudtner:2024teg}, and for scalar
theories the running of the renormalizable couplings is known up to six
loops~\cite{Bednyakov:2021ojn,Bednyakov:2025sri}.

Beyond the renormalizable level, the RGEs of higher-dimensional operators have been obtained with a
variety of techniques. The traditional diagrammatic approach, in which the coefficient of the
$1/\epsilon$ poles of the relevant Feynman diagrams is extracted in $d=4-2\epsilon$ spacetime
dimensions, was adopted for the one-loop dimension-six RGEs of the
Standard Model Effective Field Theory (SMEFT)~\cite{Buchmuller:1985jz,Grzadkowski:2010es,Brivio:2017vri,Isidori:2023pyp} in
Refs.~\cite{Jenkins:2013zja,Jenkins:2013wua,Alonso:2013hga,Alonso:2014zka}, and similarly for the
Low-Energy Effective Field Theory (LEFT)~\cite{Jenkins:2017dyc}.\footnote{Many contributions to the RGEs of operators of dimensionality
different from six have been computed in the
literature~\cite{Chankowski:1993tx,Babu:1993qv,Antusch:2001ck,Davidson:2018zuo,Liao:2016hru,Liao:2019tep,Zhang:2023kvw,Zhang:2023ndw,Chala:2021pll,DasBakshi:2022mwk,DasBakshi:2023htx,Chala:2023xjy,Jiang:2020mhe,Helset:2022pde,Assi:2023zid,Boughezal:2024zqa}, and also some
two-loop~\cite{Aebischer:2022anv,Ibarra:2024tpt,Aebischer:2025hsx,Naterop:2024cfx,Jenkins:2023bls,Jenkins:2023rtg,Born:2024mgz,DiNoi:2023ygk,DiNoi:2024ajj} and even three-loop
results~\cite{deVries:2019nsu} are known.}
RGEs can also be computed using functional
methods~\cite{Fuentes-Martin:2024agf}, as was done for the one-loop bosonic
SMEFT~\cite{Buchalla:2019wsc} and, more recently, for the complete set of two-loop bosonic SMEFT
operators~\cite{Born:2024mgz}, or by geometric arguments, in which the scalar and gauge fields are
interpreted as coordinates of a manifold and the RGEs are related to the curvature in this field
space~\cite{Helset:2022pde,Assi:2023zid,Jenkins:2023rtg,Jenkins:2023bls}.

The method employed here is instead the on-shell one reviewed in Chapter~\ref{ch:RGEmethod}, which is
particularly well suited to a general EFT. Calculations are performed
exclusively with physical external states, so that no mixing with unphysical operators can occur and
gauge invariance is preserved manifestly throughout, while the selection rules discussed in
Chapter~\ref{ch:RGEmethod} sharply restrict the allowed operator mixings and substantially reduce the
computational complexity. The results are then directly usable, without invoking field redefinitions
or the additional simplifications associated with gauge
redundancies~\cite{Baratella:2020lzz}.\footnote{See also Refs.~\cite{Chavda:2025aqm,Chavda:2025awr} for a recently
proposed connection between unitarity and renormalization flow.}

The physical basis of operators used in the following is constructed by identifying the independent
kinematic structures associated with the contact amplitudes of the
EFT~\cite{Shadmi:2018xan,Cheung:2016drk,Falkowski:2019zdo,Durieux:2019siw,Li:2022tec,DeAngelis:2022qco,Durieux:2019eor}. Within it, we compute the complete set of one-loop RGEs for all
Lagrangian parameters, including the mixing of operators of different dimensionalities. Together with
the parallel results of Refs.~\cite{Fonseca:2025zjb,Misiak:2025xzq,Fonseca:2025cls}, this concludes the program of
general one-loop RGEs for effective field theories up to mass dimension six.

The chapter is organized as follows. In Section~\ref{sec:EFT} we describe the general EFT up to mass
dimension six, introducing the renormalizable interactions and the classification of the
higher-dimensional operators. In Section~\ref{sec:gEFTexamples} we recall the ingredients of the
on-shell method that are needed in the following and work out two representative anomalous dimensions
in detail. The complete results are collected in Section~\ref{sec:gEFTresults} and are then applied to
specific theories, namely the SMEFT in Section~\ref{sec:gEFTSMEFT}, the LEFT in
Section~\ref{sec:gEFTLEFT}, and the $O(n)$ scalar theory in Section~\ref{sec:gEFTOn}. Besides
providing a valuable consistency check, these applications serve as a guide to the use of the general
results in specific theories. Section~\ref{sec:gEFTconclusions} is dedicated to our conclusions.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{General Effective Gauge Theory}
\label{sec:EFT}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

In this section, we set the stage for formulating the general EFT. 
We introduce the renormalizable
interactions and the higher-dimensional operators, together with the corresponding notation that is used in subsequent sections.
The complete Lagrangian takes the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fullEFT}
    \mathcal{L}_{\text{EFT}} = \mathcal{L}^{(4)}+\mathcal{L}^{(5)}+\mathcal{L}^{(6)}\,, 
\end{equation}
where the individual parts are defined in the following subsections in Eqs.~\eqref{eq:L4}, \eqref{eq:L5}, and \eqref{eq:L6}.


%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsection{Renormalizable Interactions}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

We start by introducing the most general Lagrangian up to mass dimension four that contains an arbitrary number of real scalar fields $\phi_a$, gauge fields with field strengths $F_{\mu\nu}^{A_\alpha}$, and left-handed Weyl spinors $\psi_i$:\footnote{For the two-component notation we follow the conventions in Ref.~\cite{Dreiner:2008tw}.}
\begin{align}\label{eq:L4}
\mathcal{L}^{(4)} &=  \mathcal{L}^{(4)}_{\text{bos}} + \mathcal{L}^{(4)}_{\text{fer}}\,,\\
    \mathcal{L}^{(4)}_{\text{bos}} &= -\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_G}\xi^{A_\alpha B_\beta} F^{A_\alpha}_{\mu\nu}F^{{B_\beta}\mu\nu}+ \sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_G} \tildee \xi^{A_\alpha B_\beta} F^{A_\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\tildee F^{{B_\beta}\,\mu\nu}  + \frac{1}{2} \eta_{ab} (D_\mu\phi)_{a}(D^\mu\phi)_b\nonumber\\
    &\quad -\Lambda-t_a \phi_a-\frac{m^2_{ab}}{2!}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}-\frac{h_{abc}}{3!}\phi_a \phi_b\phi_c
    -\frac{\lambda_{abcd}}{4!}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}\phi_{c}\phi_{d}\,,\\
    \mathcal{L}^{(4)}_{\text{fer}} &= i\, \kappa_{ij}\,  \psi^\dagger_i \overline{\sigma}^\mu (D_\mu \psi)_j - \frac{1}{2!}\left (m_{ij} \psi_i \psi_j + m_{ij}^* \psi_i^\dagger \psi_j^\dagger \right) - \frac{1}{2!}\left (y_{ija} \psi_i \psi_j + y_{ija}^* \psi_i^\dagger \psi_j^\dagger \right)\phi_a  \,.
\end{align}
Here, $\overline \sigma^\mu = (\mathbb{1}_{2\times 2}, -\vec \sigma)$, where $\vec \sigma$ are the Pauli matrices.
%
In addition to all kinetic and mass terms for scalars and fermions, the Lagrangian includes topological terms, the vacuum energy, $\Lambda$, the tadpole interactions, $\{t_a\}$, triple and quartic scalar interactions, $\{h_{abc}\}$ and $\{\lambda_{abcd}\}$, and Yukawa terms, $\{y_{ija}\}$. In particular, the real parameters $m^2_{ab}$, $h_{abc}$, and $\lambda_{abcd}$ are totally symmetric, and the same is true for the complex fermion mass matrix $m_{ij}$ and the Yukawa matrices $y_{ija}$.

The gauge group $G$ is assumed to be a direct product of an arbitrary number of simple and compact gauge groups $G_\alpha$
\begin{equation}
    G = \prod_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} G_\alpha\,.
    \label{eq:full_gauge_group}
\end{equation}
Each gauge group can be any simple Lie group (thus including $SU(n)$, $SO(n)$, or $Sp(n)$), as well as $U(1)$.

In the following, the adjoint indices of the gauge groups $G_\alpha$ are denoted by $A_\alpha,B_\alpha,\dotsc \in \{1,\dotsc,\dime G_\alpha\}$,
while scalars and fermions are taken to transform in (possibly reducible) representations $S_\alpha$ and $F_\alpha$, respectively. 
The corresponding gauge indices are
$a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},\dotsc \in \{1,\dotsc,\dime S_{\alpha}\}$ and $i_\alpha,j_\alpha,\dotsc \in \{1,\dotsc,\dime F_{\alpha}\}$, and for notational simplicity their collections over the different gauge-group factors are denoted by $a,b,\dotsc$ and $i,j,\dotsc$, respectively.
Moreover, the covariant derivatives of the scalar and fermion fields are defined by
\begin{align}
    (D_\mu\phi)_{a} &= \partial_\mu\phi_{a} -i\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} g_\alpha A^{A_\alpha}_\mu \theta^{A_\alpha}_{ab}\phi_{b}\,,\label{eq:cov_der_phi} \\
    (D_\mu\psi)_{i} &= \partial_\mu\psi_{i} -i\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} g_\alpha A^{A_\alpha}_\mu t^{A_\alpha}_{ij}\psi_{j}\label{eq:cov_der_psi}\,,
\end{align}
where $g_\alpha$ denotes the gauge couplings and $\theta^{A_\alpha}$ and $t^{A_\alpha}$ are the Hermitian generators acting on the scalar and fermion fields, respectively. They satisfy: 
\begin{gather}
    [\theta^{A_\alpha},\theta^{B_\alpha}] = i\, f^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha}\,\theta^{C_\alpha} \,, \quad
    [t^{A_\alpha},t^{B_\alpha}] = i\, f^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha}\,t^{C_\alpha} \,,
\end{gather}
%
with the totally anti-symmetric structure constants $f^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha}$. The field-strength tensors read\footnote{We use the convention $\epsilon^{0123} =-\epsilon_{0123}= 1$.}
\begin{gather}
    F^{A_\alpha}_{\mu\nu}=\partial_\mu A^{A_\alpha}_\nu - \partial_\nu A^{A_\alpha}_\mu + g_\alpha f^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha}A^{B_\alpha}_\mu A^{C_\alpha}_\nu\,,\label{eq:field_strength}\\
    \tildee F^{A_\alpha}_{\mu\nu}= \frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}  F^{A_\alpha\rho\sigma}\,.
\end{gather}
%
Finally, due to gauge invariance, the gauge kinetic and topological terms take the following form
\begin{equation}
\xi^{A_\alpha B_\beta} = \delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta^{A_\alpha B_\beta}\,,\quad \tildee \xi^{A_\alpha B_\beta} = \frac{\vartheta_\alpha g_\alpha^2}{32\pi^2} \delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta^{A_\alpha B_\beta} \,,
\end{equation}
with the adjoint indices $A_\alpha$ and $B_\beta$ belonging to non-Abelian gauge factors. 
We normalized the topological angles $\vartheta_\alpha$ in a canonical way using $g^2/(32\pi^2) \int \dd^4x\, F \tildee F \in \mathbb{Z}$.

In the case of Abelian $U(1)$ factors that lead to off-diagonal kinetic and topological terms~\cite{Holdom:1985ag,delAguila:1988jz}, the gauge couplings $g_{\alpha}$ need to be generalized to a matrix $g^{A_\alpha B_\beta}$. In order to allow for this possibility, the simple substitution
\begin{equation}
    g_\alpha \theta^{A_\alpha} \to \sum_{\beta=1}^{N_G} g^{A_\alpha B_\beta} \theta^{B_\beta}
\end{equation} 
can be made in the RGEs below,
and similarly with the fermionic generators.

For convenience, the following definitions are introduced
\begin{align}
    T^\alpha_{abcd} &= \theta^{A_\alpha}_{ab}\theta^{A_\alpha}_{cd}\,,\label{eq:T_def}\\
    Y^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab} &= \theta^{A_\alpha}_{ac}\theta^{B_\beta}_{cb}\,,\label{eq:Y_def}\\
    F^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha D_\alpha} &= f^{A_\alpha B_\alpha E_\alpha} f^{C_\alpha D_\alpha E_\alpha}\,,\label{eq:TYF_defs}
\end{align}
which occur in several of the RGEs reported in Section~\ref{sec:gEFTresults}.

\subsubsection{Collinear Anomalous Dimensions}

The infrared collinear anomalous dimensions, relevant for the multiplicative renormalization factors, are computed using the on-shell method reviewed in Chapter~\ref{ch:RGEmethod} and exploiting the fact that the stress-energy tensor does not renormalize multiplicatively, as done in \cite{Caron-Huot:2016cwu,EliasMiro:2020tdv,AccettulliHuber:2021uoa,Bresciani:2024shu,Baratella:2022nog,Aebischer:2025zxg}.
They are given by
\begin{align}
    \gamma_{c,v}^{A_\alpha B_\alpha} &= -g_\alpha^2 \left[b_{0,\alpha}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha} \,, \label{eq:coll_vector}  \\
    \gamma_{c,s}^{ab} &= -4\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} g_\alpha^2 \left[C_2(S_\alpha)\right]_{a_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}} + \frac{1}{2}\left( y_{ija}y_{jib}^*+y_{ijb}y_{jia}^* \right) \,, \label{eq:coll_scalar} \\
    \gamma_{c,f}^{ij} &= -3\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} g_\alpha^2 \left[C_2(F_\alpha)\right]_{i_\alpha j_\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}y_{ika}y^*_{kja} \,, \label{eq:coll_fermion}
\end{align}
for gauge boson, scalar, and fermion fields, respectively,
with the one-loop $\beta$-function coefficient
\begin{equation}
    \left[b_{0,\alpha}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha} = \frac{11}{3}[C_2(G_\alpha)]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha} - \frac{1}{6} [S_2(S_\alpha)]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha} -\frac{2}{3}[S_2(F_\alpha)]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha}\,.
\end{equation}
The quadratic Casimir $C_2$ and Dynkin indices $S_2$ for the different scalar and fermion representations, $S_\alpha$ and $F_\alpha$, are given by
\begin{align}
    \left[C_2(G_\alpha)\right]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha} &= f^{A_\alpha C_\alpha D_\alpha}f^{B_\alpha C_\alpha D_\alpha}\,,\\
    \left[S_2(S_\alpha)\right]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha} &= \Tr(\theta^{A_\alpha}\theta^{B_\alpha})\,, \\ 
    \left[C_2(S_\alpha)\right]_{a_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}} &= \theta^{A_\alpha}_{a_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}} \theta^{A_\alpha}_{c_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}}\,,\\
    \left[S_2(F_\alpha)\right]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha} &= \Tr(t^{A_\alpha}t^{B_\alpha})\,, \\
    \left[C_2(F_\alpha)\right]_{i_{\alpha}j_{\alpha}} &= t^{A_\alpha}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}} t^{A_\alpha}_{k_{\alpha}i_{\alpha}}\,.
\end{align}

\subsection{Higher-Dimensional Operator Classification}

A basis of physical operators at a given mass dimension can be conveniently constructed by analyzing the independent kinematic structures associated with the contact amplitudes of the EFT~\cite{Shadmi:2018xan,Cheung:2016drk,Falkowski:2019zdo,Durieux:2019siw,Li:2022tec,DeAngelis:2022qco,Durieux:2019eor}.
In the following, we perform such a construction for operators up to mass dimension six working with the massless spinor-helicity formalism of Section~\ref{sec:spinorhelicity}.

% We work with the massless spinor-helicity formalism, where a light-like momentum $p$ is decomposed in terms of the helicity spinors $\lambda$ and $\tildee \lambda$ as
% \begin{equation}
%     p_{\alpha\dot\alpha} = p_\mu \,\sigma^\mu_{\alpha\dot\alpha} = \lambda_\alpha\, \tildee \lambda_{\dot\alpha} \,.
% \end{equation}
% Here, $\sigma^\mu = (\mathbb 1_{2\times 2},\vec \sigma)$ and the spinors $\lambda_\alpha$ and $\tildee \lambda^{\dot\alpha}$ transform in the $(1/2,0)$ and $(0,1/2)$ representations of $SL(2,\mathbb C)$, respectively.
% In this framework, the Lorentz-invariant inner products are given by\footnote{We use the convention $\epsilon^{12}=\epsilon^{\dot 1 \dot 2} = -\epsilon_{12}=-\epsilon_{\dot 1 \dot 2} = 1$.}
% \begin{gather}
%     \agl{i}{j} = \lambda_i{}^\alpha \,\lambda_j{}_\alpha = \epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\, \lambda_i{}^\alpha\, \lambda_j{}^\beta = -\agl{j}{i}\,,\\
%     \sqr{i}{j} = \tildee \lambda_i{}_{\dot\alpha}\, \tildee \lambda_j{}^{\dot \alpha} = -\epsilon_{\dot\alpha\dot\beta}\, \tildee \lambda_i{}^{\dot\alpha}\, \tildee \lambda_j{}^{\dot \beta} = -\sqr{j}{i}\,.
% \end{gather}
% The Mandelstam invariants can then be expressed as 
% \begin{equation}
%     s_{ij}=2p_i\cdot p_j = \agl{i}{j}\sqr{j}{i}\,.
% \end{equation}
% Additionally, for real momenta, square and angle products are related via 
% \begin{equation}
%     \agl{i}{j} = \sqr{j}{i}^*\,.
% \end{equation}
% Such angle and square brackets are very useful when decomposing scattering amplitudes. 
In four spacetime dimensions, $n$-point amplitudes have mass dimension
\begin{equation}
    [\mathcal M_n]=4-n\,.
\end{equation}
Furthermore, contact amplitudes $\mathcal{M}$ generated by the insertion of an effective operator are polynomials in the angle and square inner products and can be schematically written as
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal M = C_i \times \mathcal K_i\,,
\end{equation}
where $C_i$ denotes the Wilson coefficient associated with the operator $\mathcal O_i$, and $\mathcal K_i$ is a kinematic polynomial, $\mathcal K_i = \mathcal K_i(\{\agl{i}{j},\sqr{i}{j}\})$. The mass dimension of $\mathcal K_i$ is fixed by the properties of the operator $\mathcal O_i$:
\begin{equation}
    [\mathcal K_i] = [\mathcal O_i] - \ell(\mathcal O_i) \ge 0\,.
\end{equation}
Here, $\ell(\mathcal O_i)$ denotes the length of the operator, namely the minimal number of fundamental fields it contains.
Different kinematic structures can be related via momentum conservation, $\sum_{i=1}^n p_i = 0$, or Schouten identities,
\begin{align}
    \agl{i}{j}\agl{k}{\ell} + \agl{k}{i}\agl{j}{\ell} + \agl{j}{k}\agl{i}{\ell} &= 0\,,\label{eq:schhoutenid1}\\
    \sqr{i}{j}\sqr{k}{\ell} + \sqr{k}{i}\sqr{j}{\ell} + \sqr{j}{k}\sqr{i}{\ell} &= 0\label{eq:schhoutenid2}\,.
\end{align}
Moreover, on-shell three-particle amplitudes vanish due to momentum conservation for real momenta or are constant (the latter possibility holds only for the cubic scalar interaction).
Nevertheless, they can be constructed by assuming complex momenta~\cite{Witten:2003nn}, and, once the particle helicities $\{h_1,h_2,h_3\}$ are fixed, they are uniquely determined by Poincaré invariance, locality, and dimensional analysis~\cite{Benincasa:2007xk}, as shown in Section~\ref{sec:3pointamplitudes}:
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal M(1^{h_1},2^{h_2},3^{h_3}) = 
    \begin{cases}
        C_{\mathsf H}\, \agl{1}{2}^{a_3} \agl{2}{3}^{a_1} \agl{3}{1}^{a_2} & \text{if }h_1+h_2+h_3<0\,,\\  C_{\mathsf A}\, \sqr{1}{2}^{-a_3} \sqr{2}{3}^{-a_1} \sqr{3}{1}^{-a_2} & \text{if }h_1+h_2+h_3>0\,,
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
    a_1 = h_1-h_2-h_3\,,\qquad
    a_2 = h_2-h_3-h_1\,,\qquad
    a_3 = h_3-h_1-h_2\,,
\end{equation}
and $C_{\mathsf H}$ and $C_{\mathsf A}$ are arbitrary coefficients with mass dimension given by
\begin{equation}
    [C_{\mathsf H}] = 1+h_1+h_2+h_3\,,\qquad 
    [C_{\mathsf A}] = 1-h_1-h_2-h_3\,.
\end{equation}

Therefore, we can classify all \emph{on-shell} independent kinematic polynomials and the corresponding \emph{physical} operators as follows.
\begin{itemize}
    \item For dimension-five operators $[\mathcal K_i] = 5 - \ell(\mathcal O_i)$:
    \begin{align}
        \ell_i &= 5
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal K_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            1
        \end{cases} 
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal O_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            \phi_1\, \phi_2\, \phi_3\, \phi_4\, \phi_5\,,
        \end{cases}
        \\
        \ell_i &= 4 
        &&
        \implies
        &
        \mathcal K_i &= 
        \begin{cases}
             \agl{1}{2}
        \end{cases}
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal O_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            \psi_1{}_L\,  \psi_2{}_L\,  \phi_3\,  \phi_4  \,,
        \end{cases}
        \\
        \ell_i &= 3 
        &&
        \implies
        &
        \mathcal K_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            \agl{1}{2}^2 \\
            \agl{1}{2}\agl{1}{3}
        \end{cases}
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal O_i &= 
        \begin{cases}
            F_1{}_L\,  F_2{}_L\,  \phi_3\,, \\
            F_1{}_L\,  \psi_2{}_L\,  \psi_3{}_L \,.
        \end{cases}
    \end{align}
    
    \item For dimension-six operators $[\mathcal K_i] = 6 -\ell(\mathcal O_i)$:
    \begin{align}
        \ell_i &= 6
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal K_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            1
        \end{cases} 
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal O_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            \phi_1\, \phi_2\, \phi_3\, \phi_4\, \phi_5\, \phi_6\,,
        \end{cases}
         \\
        \ell_i &= 5
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal K_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            \agl{1}{2}
        \end{cases}
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal O_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            \psi_1{}_L\,  \psi_2{}_L\,  \phi_3\,  \phi_4\,  \phi_5 \,,
        \end{cases}
        \\
        \ell_i &= 4
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal K_i &= 
        \begin{cases}
            \agl{1}{2}^2 \\
            \agl{1}{2}\agl{1}{3}\\
            \agl{1}{2}\agl{3}{4}\\
            \agl{1}{2}\sqr{3}{4}\\
            \agl{1}{2}\sqr{2}{3} \\
            \agl{1}{2}\sqr{1}{2}
        \end{cases}
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal O_i &= 
        \begin{cases}
            F_1{}_L\,  F_2{}_L\,  \phi_3\,  \phi_4\,, \\
            F_1{}_L\,  \psi_2{}_L\,  \psi_3{}_L\,  \phi_4 \,,\\
            \psi_1{}_L\,  \psi_2{}_L\, \psi_3{}_L\,  \psi_4{}_L\,,\\
            \psi_1{}_L\,  \psi_2{}_L\, \psi_3{}_R\,  \psi_4{}_R\,,\\
            \psi_1{}_L\,  D\phi_2\,  \psi_3{}_R\,  \phi_4\,,\\
            D\phi_1\,  D\phi_2\,  \phi_3\,  \phi_4\,.
        \end{cases}
        \\
        \ell_i &= 3
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal K_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            \agl{1}{2}\agl{2}{3}\agl{1}{3}
        \end{cases}
        &&
        \implies 
        &
        \mathcal O_i &=
        \begin{cases}
            F_1{}_L\,  F_2{}_L\,  F_3{}_L\,.
        \end{cases}
    \end{align}
\end{itemize}
The scalar and fermion fields are denoted with $\phi$ and $\psi$, respectively, while we use $F$ for the gauge field strengths. The kinematic polynomials with angle brackets substituted by square ones and vice versa correspond to Hermitian conjugate operators. 

Finally, the symmetries of the Wilson coefficients are inherited by those of the kinematic structures.
This implies that, for bosonic operators, if a kinematic polynomial is (anti-)symmetric under the exchange of two spinors, then the Wilson coefficient must also be (anti-)symmetric in the associated pair of gauge indices.
For fermionic operators, instead, Fermi--Dirac statistics requires the amplitude to be anti-symmetric under the exchange of two identical fermions, so that the above conclusion is reversed: a kinematic polynomial that is symmetric (anti-symmetric) under the exchange of two spinors must be contracted with a Wilson coefficient that is anti-symmetric (symmetric) in the associated pair of gauge indices.

In the following, we report the basis of operators adopted in this chapter.
Regarding the bosonic operators, we choose to work with Hermitian operators so that all the components of the Wilson coefficients are real.


\subsubsection{Dimension-Five Operators}

The dimension-five Lagrangian can again be split into a bosonic and a fermionic part. It takes the form
%\begingroup
%\interdisplaylinepenalty=10000
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{L}^{(5)} &= \mathcal{L}^{(5)}_{\text{bos}} + \mathcal{L}^{(5)}_{\text{fer}}\,,\label{eq:L5}\\
    \mathcal{L}^{(5)}_{\text{bos}}&=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^\alpha \left(\left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{a}\phi_{a}F^{A_\alpha}_{\mu\nu}F^{B_\beta  \mu\nu}+\left[C_{\phi \tildee F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{a}\phi_{a}F^{A_\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\tildee F^{B_\beta  \mu\nu}\right)
    \nonumber\\&\quad 
    + \left[C_{\phi^5}\right]_{abcde}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}\phi_{c}\phi_{d}\phi_{e}\,,\\
    \mathcal{L}^{(5)}_{\text{fer}} &= \left[C_{\psi^2\phi^2}\right]_{ijab} \psi_i \psi_j \phi_a \phi_b + \sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} \left[C_{\psi^2F}\right]_{ij}^{A_\alpha} \psi_i \sigma^{\mu\nu} \psi_j F_{\mu\nu}^{A_\alpha}  + \text{H.c.}\,,
\end{align}
%\endgroup\ignorespacesafterend
where $\sigma^{\mu\nu} = \frac{i}{4}(\sigma^\mu \overline \sigma^\nu - \sigma^\nu \overline \sigma^\mu)$. In particular, the fermionic operators exhibit non-trivial symmetry properties due to various Dirac relations, which are equivalently reflected in the corresponding kinematic structures. For instance, for anti-commuting spinor fields, one finds:
%
\begin{gather}
    \psi_i \psi_j = \psi_j\psi_i\,, \qquad
    \psi_i \sigma^{\mu\nu} \psi_j = -\psi_j \sigma^{\mu\nu}\psi_i\,,
\end{gather}
and analogously for the right-handed spinors. The symmetries of the dimension-five operators together with their minimal form factors are given in Table~\ref{tab:dim5gen}.\footnote{To account for symmetry factors, the following shorthand notation is introduced:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal S_{i_1\dots i_n}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{N_{i_1\dots i_n}} k_\ell !\,,
\label{eq:symm_fact}
\end{equation}
where the quantity $N_{i_1\dots i_n}$ is the number of distinct elements in the multiset of indices $I=\{i_1,\dotsc, i_n\}$ and $k_\ell$ is the multiplicity of the $\ell$-th distinct index.
By construction,
\begin{equation}
\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_{i_1\dots i_n}}k_\ell=n\,.
\end{equation}
As an example, one finds for $n=2$ and $n=3$
\begin{equation}
\mathcal S_{ij} = \begin{cases}
1 & \text{if }i\neq j, \\
2 & \text{if }i=j,
\end{cases} \qquad 
\mathcal S_{ijk} =
\begin{cases}
1 & \text{for } i,j,k \text{ all distinct}, \\
2 & \text{for exactly two equal indices}, \\
6 & \text{for } i=j=k.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}}

\input{tables/generalEFT_dim5}

\subsubsection{Dimension-Six Operators}

The dimension-six interactions are given by the following Lagrangian:
%\begingroup
%\interdisplaylinepenalty=10000
\begin{align}\label{eq:L6}
    \mathcal{L}^{(6)} &= \mathcal{L}^{(6)}_{\text{bos}}+\mathcal{L}^{(6)}_{\text{fer}}\,,\\
    \mathcal{L}^{(6)}_{\text{bos}}&=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^\alpha \left(\left[C_{\phi^2 F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}F^{A_\alpha}_{\mu\nu}F^{B_\beta  \mu\nu}+\left[C_{\phi^2 \tildee F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}F^{A_\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\tildee F^{B_\beta  \mu\nu}\right)
    \nonumber\\&\quad  \nonumber
    + \left[C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}\phi_{c}\phi_{d}\phi_{e}\phi_{f}+\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd}(D_\mu \phi)_{a}(D^\mu \phi)_{b}\phi_{c}\phi_{d}\\&\quad
    +\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\left(\left[C_{F^3}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha}F^{A_\alpha \nu}_{\mu}F^{B_\alpha \rho}_{ \nu} F^{C_\alpha \mu}_{\rho}+\left[C_{\widetilde F^3}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha}F^{A_\alpha \nu}_{\mu}F^{B_\alpha\rho}_{ \nu} \widetilde F^{C_\alpha\mu}_{\rho}\right)\,,\\
    \mathcal{L}^{(6)}_{\text{fer}} &= \bigg(\left[C_{\psi^2\phi^3}\right]_{ijabc} \psi_i \psi_j \phi_a \phi_b \phi_c + \sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} \left[C_{\psi^2 \phi F}\right]_{ija}^{A_\alpha} \psi_i \sigma^{\mu\nu} \psi_j \phi_a F_{\mu\nu}^{A_\alpha}  \nonumber \\&\quad + \left[C_{\psi^4}\right]_{ijkl} (\psi_i \psi_j)(\psi_k\psi_l) + \text{H.c.}\bigg) + \left[C_{\overline \psi^2 \psi^2}\right]_{ijkl}(\psi_i^\dagger \overline \sigma^\mu \psi_j)(\psi_k^\dagger \overline \sigma_\mu \psi_l)\nonumber \\&\quad
    + i\left[C_{D\overline \psi \psi \phi^2}\right]_{ijab}(\psi^\dagger_i \overline \sigma^\mu \psi_j)\left[(D_\mu \phi)_a \phi_b - \phi_a (D_\mu \phi)_b\right]\,.
\end{align}
%\endgroup\ignorespacesafterend
The symmetries of the corresponding Wilson coefficients are collected for bosonic and fermionic operators in Table~\ref{tab:dim6gen}, and the minimal form factors are given in Table~\ref{tab:dim6FFs}. 

\input{tables/generalEFT_dim6}
\input{tables/generalEFT_dim6FFs}

For instance, the four-fermion operator $\mathcal{O}_{\overline \psi^2 \psi^2}$ satisfies the following Fierz relation:
%
\begin{equation}
    (\psi_i^\dagger \overline \sigma_\mu \psi_j)(\psi_k^\dagger \overline \sigma^\mu \psi_l) = 2 (\psi_i^\dagger \psi_k^\dagger)( \psi_l \psi_j) \,,
\end{equation}
which leads to several symmetry relations. Furthermore, using the identity
%
\begin{equation}
    \psi_i^\dagger \overline \sigma_\mu \psi_j = - \psi_j \sigma_\mu \psi_i^\dagger \,,
\end{equation}
the operator $\mathcal{O}_{\overline \psi^2 \psi^2}$ describes four-fermion vector operators with both equal and mixed chiralities, i.e., $(\overline LL)(\overline LL)$, $(\overline RR)(\overline RR)$, and $(\overline LL)(\overline RR)$. 
Moreover, as a consequence of the Schouten identities in Eqs.~\eqref{eq:schhoutenid1} and \eqref{eq:schhoutenid2},
the Wilson coefficient associated with the operator $\mathcal O_{\psi^4}$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
    \left[C_{\psi^4}\right]_{ijkl} + \left[C_{\psi^4}\right]_{iklj} + \left[C_{\psi^4}\right]_{iljk} = 0\,,
\end{equation}
in addition to the identities reported in Table~\ref{tab:dim6gen}.

Let us further note a subtlety that involves the $D^2\phi^4$-class operator $[\mathcal O_{D^2\phi^4}]_{abcd} = (D_\mu \phi)_a (D^\mu \phi)_b \phi_c \phi_d$. 
For this, we define the following combination of Wilson coefficients:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hatCD2phi4}
\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd}=\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd}+\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{cdab}-\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{adbc}-\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{bcad}\,.
\end{equation}
Adopting a geometric approach~\cite{Helset:2022pde,Assi:2023zid,Jenkins:2023rtg,Jenkins:2023bls}, this combination can be shown to be proportional to the Riemann tensor of the scalar metric,\footnote{For a scalar metric $g_{ab}(\phi) = \delta_{ab} + 2[C_{D^2\phi^4}]_{abcd}\phi^c \phi^d$, the corresponding Riemann tensor $R_{abcd}$ is proportional to $[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}]_{acbd} = [C_{D^2\phi^4}]_{acbd}+[C_{D^2\phi^4}]_{bdac}-[C_{D^2\phi^4}]_{adbc}-[C_{D^2\phi^4}]_{bcad}$.} which implies the following symmetries
\begin{gather}
    \left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} = \left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{badc} = -\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{adcb} = -\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{cbad}\,,\\
    \left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} + \left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{acdb} + \left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{adbc} = 0\,.
\end{gather}
Furthermore, by introducing the redundant operator
\begin{equation}
\left[\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} = (\Box \phi)_a \phi_b \phi_c \phi_d
\end{equation}
and by using integration by parts, one finds the relations
\begin{align}
    \left[\mathcal O_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} - \left[\mathcal O_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{cdab} &= -\frac{1}{2}\left(
    \left[\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} + \left[\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{bacd}\right.\nonumber\\
    &\left.\qquad\,\,-\left[\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{cabd}-\left[\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{dabc}\,
    \right)
\end{align}
and
\begin{equation}
    \left[\mathcal O_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} + \left[\mathcal O_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{adbc} +
    \left[\mathcal O_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{acdb}  = -\left[\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd}\,,\label{eq:D2phi4symmetric}
\end{equation}
implying
\begin{equation}
\left[\mathcal O_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{(abcd)} = -\frac{1}{3}\left[\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{(abcd)}\,.
\end{equation}
One can then show, by using the equation of motion of the scalar field,
\begin{equation}
    (\Box \phi)_a = -t_a -m^2_{ab}\,\phi_b - \frac{1}{2!}h_{abc}\,\phi_b\phi_c - \frac{1}{3!}\lambda_{abcd}\,\phi_b\phi_c\phi_d
    -\frac{1}{2!}\left(y_{ija}\psi_i \psi_j + y_{ija}^* \psi_i^\dagger \psi_j^\dagger
    \right)
    \,,
\end{equation}
that the redundant operator $\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}$ is a linear combination of $\mathcal O_{\phi^6}$, $\mathcal O_{\phi^5}$, $\mathcal O_{\phi^4}$, $\mathcal O_{\phi^3}$, and $\mathcal O_{\psi^2\phi^3}$.
Consequently, generating $\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}$ at the one-loop level influences the RGEs of the coefficients $C_{\phi^6}$, $C_{\phi^5}$, $\lambda$, $h$, and $C_{\psi^2\phi^3}$.
To illustrate this point, we write the Wilson coefficient $[C_{D^2\phi^4}]_{abcd}$ as
\begin{equation}
    \left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} = \frac{1}{6}\left(
    2\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd}-\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{acbd}
    \right)+\left[\tildee C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd}+\left[\overline C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd}\,,
    \label{eq:decomposition}
\end{equation}
where we introduced the combinations
\begin{align}
    \left[\tildee C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} &= \left[ C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{(abcd)}\,,\label{eq:Ctilde_def}\\
    \left[\overline C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} &= \frac{1}{2}\left(
    \left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} - \left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{cdab}
    \right)\,.\label{eq:Cbar_def}
\end{align}
In this notation, one finds the following contributions from $\mathcal R_{D^2\phi^4}$
\begin{align}
    \left[\dot C_{\psi^2\phi^3}\right]_{ijabc} &\supset \frac{1}{3!}\frac{1}{2!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c\})}y_{ijd} \left(
    \frac{1}{3}\left[\dot{\tildee C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{dabc}+\left[\dot{\overline C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{dabc}
    \right)\,,\label{eq:psi2phi3<-Ctildeandbar}\\
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} &\supset \frac{1}{6!}\frac{1}{3!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})}\lambda_{abcg}\left(
    \frac{1}{3}\left[\dot{\tildee C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{gdef}+\left[\dot{\overline C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{gdef}
    \right)\,,\label{eq:phi6<-Ctildeandbar}\\
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^5}\right]_{abcde} &\supset \frac{1}{5!}\frac{1}{2!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e\})}h_{abf}\left(
    \frac{1}{3}\left[\dot{\tildee C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{fcde}+\left[\dot{\overline C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{fcde}
    \right)\,,\label{eq:phi5<-Ctildeandbar}\\
    \dot \lambda_{abcd} &\supset - \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d\})}m^2_{ae}\left(
    \frac{1}{3}\left[\dot{\tildee C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{ebcd}+\left[\dot{\overline C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{ebcd}
    \right)\,,\label{eq:phi4<-Ctildeandbar}\\
    \dot h_{abc} &\supset -  \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c\})} t_d \left(
    \frac{1}{3}\left[\dot{\tildee C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{dabc}+\left[\dot{\overline C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{dabc}
    \right)\,,\label{eq:phi3<-Ctildeandbar}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}
    \dot C = 16\pi^2\,\mu \dv[]{}{\mu}C\,,
\end{equation}
which have to be taken into account and added to the results presented in Section~\ref{sec:gEFTresults}.
With $\sum_{\sigma(I)}$ we denote the sum over all permutations of the set of indices $I$, and $\sum_{\sigma(I\times J)} = \sum_{\sigma(I)}\sum_{\sigma(J)}$.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{Method and Sample Computations}
\label{sec:gEFTexamples}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Before collecting the complete results in Section~\ref{sec:gEFTresults}, we illustrate how
they are obtained by working out two representative anomalous dimensions in detail.
The first example, the self-mixing of the $\phi^2F^2$ class, is chosen to display the
mechanics of the method: the construction of the unitarity cuts, the parameterization of the
phase space, and the extraction of the anomalous dimension as a residue.
The second example, the mixing of $\phi^2F^2$ into $\psi^2\phi^3$, is instead chosen for its
structural content: it shows how the helicity selection rules \cite{Cheung:2015aba} organize the two Wilson
coefficients $C_{\phi^2F^2}$ and $C_{\phi^2\widetilde F^2}$ into a single holomorphic
combination, a feature that is manifest on shell but obscured in the standard
Feynman-diagrammatic approach.

The on-shell method itself is discussed in Chapter~\ref{ch:RGEmethod}. Here we merely recall
the ingredients needed to follow the two computations.
At one loop and in the massless limit, the anomalous dimensions follow from the master
formulae
\begin{align}
    \left(\gamma_{i\leftarrow j}-\delta_{ij}\,\gamma_{i,\text{IR}}\right) F_i\big|_*
    &= -\frac{1}{\pi}\,(\mathcal M F_j)\big|_*\,,
    &
    \gamma_{i\leftarrow j,k}\,F_i\big|_*
    &= -\frac{1}{\pi}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial C_k}\right|_*(\mathcal M F_j)\,,
    \label{eq:masterformula}
\end{align}
valid for a single and a double operator insertion, respectively.
Here $F_i(\vec n;q) = \mel{\vec n}{\mathcal O_i(q)}{0}$ denotes the form factor of the operator
$\mathcal O_i$ evaluated on an outgoing on-shell state $\bra{\vec n}$, and the symbol ``$*$''
indicates the Gaussian fixed point, where all Wilson coefficients vanish.
The right-hand sides involve the convolution of a tree-level form factor with a tree-level
amplitude, which amounts to a sum of two-particle unitarity cuts,
\begin{align}
    (\mathcal M F_j)(1,\dotsc,n) &=
    \sum_{k=2}^{n}\,\sum_{\{x,y\}} \int \text{dLIPS}_2 \sum_{h_x,h_y}
    F_j(x^{h_x},y^{h_y},k+1,\dotsc,n)\,
    \mathcal M(1,\dotsc,k;x^{h_x},y^{h_y})\nonumber \\
    &\quad + \text{permutations}\,,
    \label{eq:cutconvolution}
\end{align}
where $\mathcal M(\vec n;\vec m) = \mel{\vec n}{\mathcal M}{\vec m}$ and $\text{dLIPS}_2$ is
the two-body Lorentz-invariant phase-space measure.
The infrared anomalous dimension $\gamma_{i,\text{IR}}$ results from soft and collinear particle emissions~\cite{Sterman:2002qn,Becher:2009cu,Chiu:2009mg}.
The soft divergences are isolated by the Stokes procedure \cite{Mastrolia:2009dr} described in Chapter~\ref{ch:ALP_RGEs} and below, which makes the
non-rational terms manifest and thus allows them to be discarded, since they cannot
contribute to the ultraviolet running.
The collinear anomalous dimensions $\gamma_c$ depend only on the external states associated
with $\mathcal O_i$ and are collected in Eqs.~\eqref{eq:coll_vector}--\eqref{eq:coll_fermion}.

The phase-space integrals are evaluated using the Stokes parameterization
\cite{Mastrolia:2009dr}, which is particularly efficient in the presence of infrared
divergences, as shown in Chapter~\ref{ch:ALP_RGEs}.
The virtual spinors are expressed in terms of two external ones as
\begin{equation}
    \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_x \\ \lambda_y \end{pmatrix}
     =
   \frac{1}{\sqrt{1+ z \bar{z}}}
   \begin{pmatrix} 1 & \bar{z} \\ -z & 1 \end{pmatrix}
   \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_{a} \\ \lambda_{b} \end{pmatrix}
    \,, \qquad
    \begin{pmatrix} \tildee\lambda_x \\ \tildee\lambda_y \end{pmatrix}
     =
   \frac{1}{\sqrt{1+ z \bar{z}}}
   \begin{pmatrix} 1 & z \\ -\bar z & 1 \end{pmatrix}
   \begin{pmatrix} \tildee\lambda_{a} \\ \tildee\lambda_{b} \end{pmatrix}\,,
   \label{eq:stokesparam}
\end{equation}
such that $p_x+p_y = p_a+p_b$, and, accordingly,
\begin{equation}
    \int \text{dLIPS}_2 = -\frac{1}{8 \pi} \oint \frac{\text{d}z}{2\pi i}
    \int \frac{\text{d}\bar{z}}{(1 + z \bar{z})^2}\,.
    \label{eq:dlips}
\end{equation}

In all the diagrams that follow, red blobs denote tree-level form factors with an
insertion of a higher-dimensional operator, blue blobs denote tree-level amplitudes
built solely from the marginal interactions of $\mathcal L^{(4)}$, and dashed gray lines
indicate two-particle unitarity cuts.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Bosonic Self-Mixing: $\phi^2 F^2 \leftarrow \phi^2 F^2$}{Bosonic Self-Mixing: phi2F2 from phi2F2}}
\label{sec:ex_phi2F2}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

As a first illustration we compute the self-mixing of the dimension-six operator
$\mathcal O_{\phi^2F^2}$, whose symmetry properties and minimal form factor are given in
Tables~\ref{tab:dim6gen} and \ref{tab:dim6FFs}.
Following Eq.~\eqref{eq:masterformula}, the master formula can be written diagrammatically as
\begin{align}
&-\pi \left(  \gamma_{\phi^2F^2\leftarrow \phi^2F^2}-\gamma_{\phi^2F^2,\text{IR}}  \right)

		\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]

                    \begin{feynman}[small]
                    \node [ampblob] (a);
                    \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(2_b\)};
                    \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(1_a\)} ;
                    \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-y_d\)} ;
                    \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-x_c\)};
                    \diagram*{
                        (a) -- [scalar] (b),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (d),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                    };
                    \end{feynman}
                    \end{tikzpicture}\nonumber \\
&\qquad +
                    \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b\}\times \{A_\alpha,B_\beta\})}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G}
                    \sum_{h_y=\pm}
                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                    \begin{feynman}[small]
                    \node [ffblob] (a);
                    \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(y_{C_\gamma}^{h_y}\)};
                    \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(x_c\)} ;
                    \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(3_{A_\alpha}^-\)} ;
                    \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_a\)};
                    \diagram*{
                        (a) -- [photon] (b),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                        (a) -- [photon] (d),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                    };
                    \end{feynman}
                    \end{tikzpicture}
                    \cutline
                    \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                        \begin{feynman}[small]
                        \node [ampblob] (a);
                        \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(4_{B_\beta}^-\)};
                        \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(2_b\)} ;
                        \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-y_{C_\gamma}^{-h_y}\)} ;
                        \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-x_c\)};
                        \diagram*{
                            (a) -- [photon] (b),
                            (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                            (a) -- [photon] (d),
                            (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                        };
                        \end{feynman}
                        \end{tikzpicture}
                        \label{eq:diag-master-formula}
\end{align}
The $\delta_{\alpha\beta}$ in the first term ensures that the four-vector amplitude is
non-vanishing only if the simple gauge group associated with each gauge boson is the same,
while the sum
$\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b\}\times \{A_\alpha,B_\beta\})} = \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b\})}\sum_{\sigma(\{A_\alpha,B_\beta\})}$
permutes the external scalars and gauge bosons.

The first cut of Eq.~\eqref{eq:diag-master-formula} is given by
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{C}_1 &= \sum_{h_x,h_y=\pm}
            \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
            \begin{feynman}[small]
            \node [ffblob] (a);
            \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(y_{D_\alpha}^{h_y}\)};
            \vertex [above right=1.5cm  of a] (c) {\(x_{C_\alpha}^{h_x}\)} ;
            \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(2_b\)} ;
            \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_a\)};
            \diagram*{
                (a) -- [photon] (b),
                (a) -- [photon] (c),
                (a) -- [scalar] (d),
                (a) -- [scalar] (e),
            };
            \end{feynman}
            \end{tikzpicture}
            \times
            \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ampblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(4_{B_\alpha}^{-}\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(3_{A_\alpha}^{-}\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-y_{D_\alpha}^{-h_y}\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-x_{C_\alpha}^{-h_x}\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [photon] (b),
                    (a) -- [photon] (c),
                    (a) -- [photon] (d),
                    (a) -- [photon] (e),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \nonumber \\&
                =
                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
            \begin{feynman}[small]
            \node [ffblob] (a);
            \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(y_{D_\alpha}^{-}\)};
            \vertex [above right=1.5cm  of a] (c) {\(x_{C_\alpha}^{-}\)} ;
            \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(2_b\)} ;
            \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_a\)};
            \diagram*{
                (a) -- [photon] (b),
                (a) -- [photon] (c),
                (a) -- [scalar] (d),
                (a) -- [scalar] (e),
            };
            \end{feynman}
            \end{tikzpicture}
            \times
            \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ampblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(4_{B_\alpha}^{-}\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(3_{A_\alpha}^{-}\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-y_{D_\alpha}^{+}\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-x_{C_\alpha}^{+}\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [photon] (b),
                    (a) -- [photon] (c),
                    (a) -- [photon] (d),
                    (a) -- [photon] (e),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}
\end{align}
where only the helicity configuration $(h_x,h_y)=(-,-)$ contributes, all the other
combinations giving a vanishing amplitude.
The amplitude is given by the Parke--Taylor formula~\cite{Parke:1986gb}.\footnote{In order to
relate amplitudes with incoming and outgoing states, we exploit crossing symmetry, with the
following convention for the spinors associated with flipped momenta:
$\lambda_{-p} = i \lambda_p$ and $\tildee \lambda_{-p} = i \tildee \lambda_p$.}
Together with the form factor of Table~\ref{tab:dim6FFs}, the final expression reads
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{C}_1 = 8 g_\alpha^2 \left[C_{\phi^2 F^2}\right]^{C_\alpha D_\alpha}_{ab}
    \frac{\agl{3}{4}^3 \agl{x}{y}}{\agl{3}{x}\agl{3}{y}\agl{4}{x}\agl{4}{y}}
    \left(F^{A_\alpha C_\alpha B_\alpha D_\alpha}\agl{3}{4}\agl{x}{y}
    - F^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha D_\alpha}\agl{3}{x}\agl{4}{y}\right)\,,
\end{equation}
with $F^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha D_\alpha} = f^{A_\alpha B_\alpha E_\alpha} f^{C_\alpha D_\alpha E_\alpha}$.
By parameterizing the internal spinors $(x,y)$ as linear combinations of the spinors $(3,4)$
as in Eq.~\eqref{eq:stokesparam}, we find
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{C}_1(z,\bar z) =
    8\frac{1+z\bar z}{z\bar z}{g^2_\alpha }{\left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]_{ab}^{C_\alpha D_\alpha}}
    \agl{3}{4}^2\left[z\bar z F^{A_\alpha B_\alpha C_\alpha D_\alpha }
    -(1+z\bar z)F^{A_\alpha C_\alpha B_\alpha D_\alpha }\right]\,.
\end{equation}

The second cut of Eq.~\eqref{eq:diag-master-formula} is
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{C}_2 &= \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ffblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(y_d\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm  of a] (c) {\(x_c\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(4_{B_\beta}^-\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(3_{A_\alpha}^-\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [scalar] (b),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                    (a) -- [photon] (d),
                    (a) -- [photon] (e),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \times
                                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                    \begin{feynman}[small]
                    \node [ampblob] (a);
                    \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(2_b\)};
                    \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(1_a\)} ;
                    \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-y_d\)} ;
                    \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-x_c\)};
                    \diagram*{
                        (a) -- [scalar] (b),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (d),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                    };
                    \end{feynman}
                    \end{tikzpicture}
                    \nonumber \\
    &= -2 \mathcal S_{\alpha\beta} \left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{cd}\agl{3}{4}^2
    \nonumber \\
    & \quad \times
    \left[
    \sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G} g_\gamma^2 \left(
    \frac{s_{1y}-s_{1x}}{s_{12}}T^\gamma_{abcd}- \frac{s_{12}+s_{1y}}{s_{1x}}T^\gamma_{acbd}
    - \frac{s_{12}+s_{1x}}{s_{1y}}T^\gamma_{adbc}
    \right)
    -\lambda_{abcd}
    \right]\,,
\end{align}
with $T^\gamma_{abcd} = \theta^{A_\gamma}_{ab}\theta^{A_\gamma}_{cd}$.
After parameterizing $(x,y)$ in terms of $(1,2)$, one finds
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{C}_2(z,\bar z) &= 2 \mathcal S_{\alpha\beta}
    \left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{cd}\agl{3}{4}^2
    \nonumber \\
    & \quad  \times
    \left[
    \sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G} g_\gamma^2
    \left(
    \frac{-1+z\bar z}{1+z\bar z}T^\gamma_{abcd}
    +
    \frac{2+z\bar z}{z\bar z}T^\gamma_{acbd}
    +
    (1+2z\bar z)T^\gamma_{adbc}
    \right)
    +
    \lambda_{abcd}
    \right]\,.
\end{align}

Finally, the third cut of Eq.~\eqref{eq:diag-master-formula} is given by
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{C}_3 &=
    \sum_{h_y=\pm}
                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                    \begin{feynman}[small]
                    \node [ffblob] (a);
                    \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(y_{C_\gamma}^{h_y}\)};
                    \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(x_c\)} ;
                    \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(3_{A_\alpha}^-\)} ;
                    \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_a\)};
                    \diagram*{
                        (a) -- [photon] (b),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                        (a) -- [photon] (d),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                    };
                    \end{feynman}
                    \end{tikzpicture}
                    \times
                    \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                        \begin{feynman}[small]
                        \node [ampblob] (a);
                        \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(4_{B_\beta}^-\)};
                        \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(2_b\)} ;
                        \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-y_{C_\gamma}^{-h_y}\)} ;
                        \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-x_c\)};
                        \diagram*{
                            (a) -- [photon] (b),
                            (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                            (a) -- [photon] (d),
                            (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                        };
                        \end{feynman}
                        \end{tikzpicture}
                        \nonumber\\
    &=
                        \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                    \begin{feynman}[small]
                    \node [ffblob] (a);
                    \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(y_{C_\gamma}^{-}\)};
                    \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(x_c\)} ;
                    \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(3_{A_\alpha}^-\)} ;
                    \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_a\)};
                    \diagram*{
                        (a) -- [photon] (b),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                        (a) -- [photon] (d),
                        (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                    };
                    \end{feynman}
                    \end{tikzpicture}
                    \times
                    \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                        \begin{feynman}[small]
                        \node [ampblob] (a);
                        \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(4_{B_\beta}^-\)};
                        \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(2_b\)} ;
                        \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-y_{C_\gamma}^{+}\)} ;
                        \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-x_c\)};
                        \diagram*{
                            (a) -- [photon] (b),
                            (a) -- [scalar] (c),
                            (a) -- [photon] (d),
                            (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                        };
                        \end{feynman}
                        \end{tikzpicture}
                        \nonumber\\
                        &=
    4 \mathcal S_{\gamma\alpha} \left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha C_\gamma}_{ac}
    \frac{\agl{3}{y}^2 \agl{4}{x}\sqr{y}{x}}{\agl{2}{x}\agl{2}{y}\sqr{4}{2}\sqr{x}{2}}  g_\beta
    \bigg[
    -(1-\delta_{\beta\gamma})g_\gamma s_{2x}Y^{B_\beta C_\gamma}_{bc}
    \nonumber \\
    &\quad
    +
    \delta_{\beta\gamma}g_\beta \left(
    s_{2y}Y^{B_\beta C_\beta}_{bc}
    -
    s_{24} Y^{C_\beta B_\beta}_{bc}
    \right)
    \bigg]\,,
\end{align}
with $Y^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab} = \theta^{A_\alpha}_{ac}\theta^{B_\beta}_{cb}$.
Again, only $h_y=-$ gives a non-vanishing contribution.
The parameterization of $(x,y)$ in terms of $(2,4)$ yields the rational function
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{C}_3 (z,\bar z)
    &=
    4 \mathcal S_{\gamma\alpha} \left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha C_\gamma}_{ac}
    \frac{(z\agl{2}{3}+\agl{3}{4})^2}{z\bar z (1+z\bar z)}g_\beta
    \bigg[
    (1-\delta_{\beta\gamma})g_\gamma z\bar z  Y^{B_\beta C_\gamma}_{bc}
    \nonumber \\
    &\quad
    +
    \delta_{\beta\gamma }g_\beta
    \left(
    -
    Y^{B_\beta C_\beta}_{bc}
    +
    (1+z\bar z)Y^{C_\beta B_\beta}_{bc}
    \right)
    \bigg]\,.
\end{align}

The evaluation of the right-hand side of Eq.~\eqref{eq:diag-master-formula} is then achieved
by applying the Stokes integration procedure to the function
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{C}(z,\bar z) = \frac{1}{2}\delta_{\alpha\beta} \mathcal{C}_1 (z,\bar z)
    + \frac{1}{2} \mathcal{C}_2 (z,\bar z)
    + \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b\}\times \{A_\alpha,B_\beta\})}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G} \mathcal{C}_3 (z,\bar z)\,,
\end{equation}
where the factors of $1/2$ account for the integration over indistinguishable particles.
The integral over $\bar z$ generates the rational contribution
\begin{align}
    G_{\rm rat}(z,\bar z) &= \text{Rat} \int \frac{\text{d}\bar z}{(1+z\bar z)^2}\mathcal{C}(z,\bar z) \nonumber \\
    &= \mathcal S_{\alpha\beta} \left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{cd}\agl{3}{4}^2
    \frac{1}{z(1+z\bar z)^2}\bigg[
    \sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G} g_\gamma^2 [-z\bar z T^\gamma_{abcd}+(1+z\bar z ) (T^\gamma_{acbd}+T^\gamma_{adbc})]
    \nonumber \\ &\quad
    -(1+z\bar z)\lambda_{abcd}
    \bigg]
    +
    \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b\}\times \{A_\alpha,B_\beta\})}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G}
    2 \mathcal S_{\gamma\alpha} \left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha C_\gamma}_{ac}
    \frac{(z\agl{2}{3}+\agl{3}{4})^2}{z (1+z\bar z)^2 }g_\beta \nonumber \\
    &\quad \times \left[
    -(1-\delta_{\beta\gamma})g_\gamma Y^{B_\beta C_\gamma}_{bc} - \delta_{\beta\gamma}g_\beta
    \left(
    (3+2z\bar z)Y^{B_\beta C_\beta}_{bc}-2(1+z\bar z)Y^{C_\beta B_\beta}_{bc}
    \right)
    \right]\,.
\end{align}
Lastly, the phase-space integration is given by the residue of $G(z,\bar z)$ at $z_0=0$:
\begin{align}
\int \text{dLIPS}_2 \, \mathcal{C} &= -\frac{1}{8\pi} \Res_{(z_0,\bar z_0)=(0,0)} G_{\rm rat}(z,\bar z) \nonumber \\
&=
\frac{1}{8\pi} \mathcal S_{\alpha\beta} \left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{cd}\agl{3}{4}^2
\bigg[
- \sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G} g_\gamma^2  \left(T^\gamma_{acbd}+T^\gamma_{adbc}\right)+\lambda_{abcd}
\bigg]\nonumber \\
&\quad +
\frac{1}{4\pi}
\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b\}\times \{A_\alpha,B_\beta\})}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G}
\mathcal S_{\gamma\alpha} \left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha C_\gamma}_{ac}
   g_\beta
   \bigg[
   (1-\delta_{\beta\gamma})g_\gamma Y^{B_\beta C_\gamma}_{bc}
   \nonumber \\ &\quad +
    \delta_{\beta\gamma}g_\beta  \left(
    3 Y^{B_\beta C_\beta}_{bc}- 2Y^{C_\beta B_\beta}_{bc}
    \right)
   \bigg]\,.
\end{align}
The resulting anomalous dimension is the one reported in Eq.~\eqref{eq:phi2F2_phi2F2}.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Holomorphy: $\psi^2 \phi^3 \leftarrow \phi^2 F^2$}{Holomorphy: psi2phi3 from phi2F2}}
\label{sec:ex_psi2phi3}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

To illustrate how non-trivial structural features of the anomalous-dimension matrix arise
naturally in the on-shell formalism, we now consider the renormalization of
$\mathcal{O}_{\psi^2\phi^3}$ induced by $\mathcal{O}_{\phi^2 F^2}$ and
$\mathcal{O}_{\phi^2 \widetilde F^2}$.
As we shall see, in the final result the two Wilson coefficients combine into the single
self-dual structure $C_{\phi^2 F^2} + i C_{\phi^2 \widetilde F^2}$, enforced by the helicity
selection rules of Ref.~\cite{Cheung:2015aba}.
This organization is automatic in the on-shell framework but highly non-trivial in standard
Feynman-diagram-based calculations, as we discuss at the end of this subsection.

The anomalous dimension receives contributions from three unitarity cuts:
\begin{align}
\mathcal C_1 \ &= \
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ffblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(x_{A_\alpha}^-\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(y_{B_\beta}^-\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(2_b\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_{a}\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [photon] (b),
                    (a) -- [photon] (c),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (d),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \,
	\cutline
	\,
    \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ampblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(3_c\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(5_j^-\)} ;
                \vertex [right=1.2cm of a] (f) {\(4_i^-\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-x_{A_\alpha}^+\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-y_{B_\beta}^+\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [scalar] (b),
                    (a) -- [fermion] (c),
                    (a) -- [photon] (d),
                    (a) -- [photon] (e),
                    (a) -- [fermion] (f),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}\,,
                \\
                \mathcal C_2 \ &= \
                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ffblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(x_{d}\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(y_{A_\alpha}^-\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(3_c\)} ;
                \vertex [left=1.2cm of a] (f) {\(2_b\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_{a}\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [scalar] (b),
                    (a) -- [photon] (c),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (d),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (f),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \,
	\cutline
	\,
    \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ampblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(4_i^-\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(5_j^-\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-x_d\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-y^+_{A_\alpha}\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [fermion] (b),
                    (a) -- [fermion] (c),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (d),
                    (a) -- [photon] (e),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}\,,
                \\
                \mathcal C_3 \ &= \
                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ffblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(x_{k}^+\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(y_{A_\alpha}^-\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(4_i^-\)} ;
                \vertex [left=1.2cm of a] (f) {\(2_b\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_{a}\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (b) -- [fermion] (a),
                    (a) -- [photon] (c),
                    (a) -- [fermion] (d),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (e),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (f),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \,
	\cutline
	\,
    \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ampblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(3_c\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(5_j^-\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(-x_k^-\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(-y^+_{A_\alpha}\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [scalar] (b),
                    (a) -- [fermion] (c),
                    (a) -- [fermion] (d),
                    (a) -- [photon] (e),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}\,.
\end{align}
Arrows on the fermion lines denote the chirality flow.
Those shown here are the only contributions, since all other possible helicity assignments for
the virtual particles lead to vanishing amplitudes or form factors.
By using again the Stokes integration method, the resulting unitarity cuts are
given by
\begin{align}
    \mathcal C_1 &= 0\,,
    \\
    \mathcal C_2 &= -\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c\})}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_G}
    g_\alpha g_\beta \mathcal S_{\alpha\beta}
    \left(\left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab}
    + i \left[C_{\phi^2\widetilde F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab} \right)\nonumber \\
    &\quad\times\theta^{B_\beta}_{cd}\left(t^{A_\alpha}_{kj}y_{ikd}+t^{A_\alpha}_{ki}y_{jkd}\right)\agl{4}{5}+\dotsc \,,
    \\
    \mathcal C_3 &= \frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_G}g_\alpha g_\beta \mathcal S_{\alpha\beta}
    \left(\left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab}
    + i \left[C_{\phi^2\widetilde F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab} \right)
    t^{B_\beta}_{ki}\left(2t^{A_\alpha}_{lj}y_{klc}-t^{A_\alpha}_{lk}y_{jlc}\right)\agl{4}{5} \,,
\end{align}
where the dots in the expression of $\mathcal C_2$ refer to non-local contributions, which are
anti-symmetric under the exchange of both the scalar indices $\{a,b,c\}$ and their associated
momenta $\{p_1,p_2,p_3\}$, and therefore cannot contribute to the anomalous dimension of
$\mathcal O_{\psi^2\phi^3}$.

Then, by summing over all permutations of external particles and the gauge groups associated
with the virtual gauge bosons, and using the master formula in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:masterformula}, one finds
\begin{align}
    \left[\dot C_{\psi^2\phi^3}\right]_{ijabc} &= \frac{1}{3}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c\}\times\{i,j\})}
    \sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^\alpha g_\alpha g_\beta
    \left(\left[C_{\phi^2F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab}
    + i \left[C_{\phi^2\widetilde F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab} \right) \nonumber \\
    &\times t^{A_\alpha}_{ki}\left(t^{B_\beta}_{lk}y_{jlc}-2t^{B_\beta}_{lj}y_{klc}
    +2\theta^{B_\beta}_{cd}y_{jkd}\right)\,.\label{eq:partres}
\end{align}
Finally, one can use the identity
\begin{equation}
    y_{ijb}\theta^{A_\alpha}_{ba}  + y_{ika}t^{A_\alpha}_{kj} + y_{jka}t^{A_\alpha}_{ki} = 0\,,
    \label{eq:gaugeinvid}
\end{equation}
which follows from requiring the gauge invariance of the tree-level amplitude
\begin{equation*}
                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
                \begin{feynman}[small]
                \node [ampblob] (a);
                \vertex [below right=1.5cm of a] (b) {\(3_i^-\)};
                \vertex [above right=1.5cm of a] (c) {\(4_j^-\)} ;
                \vertex [below left=1.5cm of a] (d) {\(2_a\)} ;
                \vertex [above left=1.5cm of a] (e) {\(1_{A_\alpha}^+\)};
                \diagram*{
                    (a) -- [fermion] (b),
                    (a) -- [fermion] (c),
                    (a) -- [scalar] (d),
                    (a) -- [photon] (e),
                };
                \end{feynman}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                =
                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
	\begin{feynman}[small]
		\vertex[](a);
		\vertex[above left =  of a](b){\(1_{A_\alpha}^+\)};
		\vertex[right = of a](f);
        \vertex[below left =  of a](c){\(2_a\)};
		\vertex[below right = of f](d){\(3_i^-\)};
		\vertex[above right = of f](e){\(4_j^-\)};
		\diagram*{
			(a) -- [photon](b),
			(a) -- [scalar](c),
			(f) -- [scalar,edge label=\(b\)](a),
			(f) -- [fermion](e),
			(f) -- [fermion](d),
		};
	\end{feynman}
	\end{tikzpicture}
    +
    \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
	\begin{feynman}[small]
		\vertex[](a);
		\vertex[above right =  of a](b){\(4_j^-\)};
		\vertex[above left =  of a](d){\(1_{A_\alpha}^+\)};
		\vertex[below = of a](f);
		\vertex[below left = of f](g){\(2_a\)};
		\vertex[below right = of f](h){\(3_i^-\)};
		\diagram*{
			(a) -- [fermion](b),
			(a) -- [photon](d),
			(f) -- [fermion,edge label=\(k\)](a),
			(g) -- [scalar](f),
			(f) -- [fermion](h),
		};
	\end{feynman}
	\end{tikzpicture}
    +
                \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={(current bounding box.center)}]
\begin{feynman}[small]
    \vertex[](a);
    \vertex[above left  = of a](d){\(1_{A_\alpha}^+\)};
    \vertex[above right = of a](h){\(4_j^-\)};
    \vertex[below = of a](f);
    \vertex[below left  = of f](g){\(2_a\)};
    \vertex[below right = of f](b){\(3_i^-\)};

    \diagram*{
        (a) -- [fermion] (b),
        (a) -- [photon] (d),
        (f) -- [fermion, edge label=\(k\)](a),
        (g) -- [scalar](f),
    };

    \draw[fermion, preaction={draw=white, line width=5pt}] (f) -- (h);

\end{feynman}
\end{tikzpicture}
                \,,
\end{equation*}
that is, by enforcing its independence from the reference momentum associated with the gauge
boson, to simplify Eq.~\eqref{eq:partres}, and in particular to write it as a function of only
$t^{A_\alpha}_{ki}t^{B_\beta}_{lk}y_{jlc}$, as reported in the final result of
Eq.~\eqref{psi2phi3_phi2F2}.

A noteworthy feature of the result in Eq.~\eqref{psi2phi3_phi2F2} (and already of the unitarity
cuts $\mathcal C_2$ and $\mathcal C_3$) is that the two Wilson coefficients enter only through
the self-dual combination
\begin{equation}\label{eq:holcomb}
    \left[C_{\phi^2 F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab}
    + i \left[C_{\phi^2 \widetilde F^2}\right]^{A_\alpha B_\beta}_{ab}\,.
\end{equation}
This is enforced by the helicity selection rules of Ref.~\cite{Cheung:2015aba}.
To each $n$-point operator one associates the holomorphic weights $(w,\overline w)=(n-h,n+h)$,
where $h$ is the total helicity of the corresponding form factor.
The associated non-renormalization theorem states that the contribution of $\mathcal O_j$ to the
running of $C_i$ vanishes unless $w_i\geq w_j$ and $\overline w_i\geq\overline w_j$.
In our case, $\mathcal O_{\psi^2\phi^3}$ has weights $(6,4)$, while the self-dual projection
$\phi^2 F^2\propto\mathcal O_{\phi^2 F^2}+i\mathcal O_{\phi^2\widetilde F^2}$ has $(6,2)$
(allowed) and the anti-self-dual projection
$\phi^2 \overline F^2  \propto\mathcal O_{\phi^2 F^2}-i\mathcal O_{\phi^2\widetilde F^2}$
has $(2,6)$ (forbidden; it contributes only to $\dot C^*_{\psi^2\phi^3}$).
Hence the combination in Eq.~\eqref{eq:holcomb}.

In the standard Feynman-diagrammatic approach, by contrast, divergences proportional to
$C_{\phi^2 F^2}$ and $C_{\phi^2\widetilde F^2}$ arise from topologically distinct diagrams and
are absorbed into separate counterterms of the enlarged Green basis.
As a result, the combination in Eq.~\eqref{eq:holcomb} only re-emerges after projection onto the
physical basis via field redefinitions.
The same pattern underlies more generally the holomorphic structure in the SMEFT RGEs observed
in Ref.~\cite{Alonso:2014rga} and that can be noted in the results of the general EFT reported in Section~\ref{sec:gEFTresults}, which, in the standard formalism, appears after non-trivial
cancellations between Green-basis contributions, while it is manifest on shell.

\input{images/graph}

\section{Results\label{sec:gEFTresults}}

This section contains the complete RGEs for the general EFT defined in Eq.~\eqref{eq:fullEFT}.
The contributions are ordered according to their mass dimension, starting from higher-dimensional operators.

A graphical representation of the mixing pattern involving dimension-five and -six bosonic operators is depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:graph}. 
The figure can also be used to navigate the individual results of the bosonic anomalous-dimension matrix elements.

\subsection{Running of Dimension-Six Operators}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6$}{phi6} Class}

%In addition to the results presented below, one needs to supplement the RGEs of the $\phi^6$-class Wilson coefficients with the expressions given in Eq.~\eqref{eq:phi6<-Ctildeandbar}.
%The explicit expressions for $\dot{\tildee C}_{D^2\phi^4}$ and $\dot{\overline C}_{D^2\phi^4}$ can be obtained using Eq.~\eqref{eq:decomposition} and the RGEs in Section~\ref{sec:D2phi4RGE}.

In addition to the results presented below, one needs to supplement the RGEs of the $\phi^6$-class Wilson coefficients with the expressions given in Eq.~\eqref{eq:phi6<-Ctildeandbar}.
The explicit expressions for $\dot{\tildee C}_{D^2\phi^4}$ and $\dot{\overline C}_{D^2\phi^4}$ can be obtained using their definitions in Eqs.~\eqref{eq:Ctilde_def} and \eqref{eq:Cbar_def}, together with the RGEs for $C_{D^2\phi^4}$ reported in this section.

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6 \leftarrow \phi^6$}{phi6fromphi6}.}
\label{par:phi6<-phi6}

\begin{align}
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} &= \frac{1}{2!4!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})}\bigg[\lambda_{efgh}+2\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} g_\alpha^2 T^\alpha_{eghf}\bigg]\left[ C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdgh}\nonumber \\
    & +\frac{1}{5!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})} \gamma_{c,s}^{ag}\left[ C_{\phi^6}\right]_{gbcdef}
    \label{eq:phi6<-phi6}\,.
\end{align}
Here, $\gamma_{c,s}$ denotes the collinear anomalous dimension of scalar fields, as defined in Eq.~\eqref{eq:coll_scalar}.

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6 \leftarrow D^2\phi^4$.}{phi6fromD2phi4}}
\label{par:phi6<-D2phi4}

\begin{align}
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} &= \frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{6!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})}\lambda_{ghab}\bigg[\lambda_{ihcd}\left[ C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{gief}\nonumber\\
    &+\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{icde}\left(\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{ihgf}+\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{ighf}-\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{hgif}\right)\bigg]\nonumber\\
    &- \frac{6}{6!} \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_G} g_\alpha^2 g_\beta^2 T^\alpha_{fhei} T^\beta_{dicg}\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abgh}\nonumber\\
    &-\frac{3}{6!} \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} g_\alpha^2 \lambda_{abhi} T^\alpha_{cidg} \left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{ghef}\,.
    \label{eq:phi6<-D2phi4}
\end{align}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6 \leftarrow \phi^5\times\phi^5$.}{phi6fromphi5xphi5}}
\label{par:phi6<-phi5xphi5}

\begin{equation}
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} = -\frac{1}{2}\frac{(5!)^2}{6!(3!)^2}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})}\left[ C_{\phi^5}\right]_{abcgh}\left[ C_{\phi^5}\right]_{ghdef}\,.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6 \leftarrow \phi F^2\times\phi^5$.}{phi6fromphiF2xphi5}}
\label{par:phi6<-phiF2xphi5}

\begin{equation}
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} = -\frac{5!}{6!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})} \sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} \sum_{\beta=1}^{\alpha} g_\alpha g_\beta \left[C_{\phi^5}\right]_{abcdg} Y_{eg}^{A_\alpha B_\beta} \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{f}^{A_\alpha B_\beta}\,.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6 \leftarrow \phi^2 F^2$}{phi6fromphi2F2}.}
\label{par:phi6<-phi2F2}

\begin{align}
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} &=
    \frac{2}{6!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})} \Bigg[\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} \sum_{\beta=1}^{\alpha} g_\alpha g_\beta \lambda_{abcg} Y_{dg}^{A_\alpha B_\beta} \left[C_{\phi^2 F^2}\right]_{ef}^{A_\alpha B_\beta}\nonumber\\
    &- 6 \sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\alpha}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G} g_\alpha g_\beta g_\gamma^2 \,Y_{ce}^{A_\alpha C_\gamma} Y_{df}^{B_\beta C_\gamma} \left[C_{\phi^2 F^2}\right]_{ab}^{A_\alpha B_\beta}\Bigg]\,.
    \label{eq:phi6<-phi2F2}
\end{align}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6 \leftarrow \phi F^2 \times \phi F^2$}{phi6fromphiF2xphiF2}.}
\label{par:phi6<-phiF2xphiF2}

\begin{align}
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} &=
    \frac{1}{6!} 
    \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})}\Bigg\{
    -24\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\alpha}
    \sum_{\gamma=1}^{\beta}\sum_{\delta=1}^{N_G} 
    g_\alpha g_\gamma g_\delta^2 \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{a}^{A_\alpha B_\beta}
    \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{b}^{B_\beta C_\gamma}\nonumber\\
    &\times Y_{cd}^{C_\gamma D_\delta}
    Y_{ef}^{D_\delta A_\alpha}-24 \sum_{\beta=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\beta}
    \sum_{\gamma=1}^{\alpha}\sum_{\delta=1}^{N_G} g_\beta g_\gamma g_\delta^2 
    \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{a}^{A_\alpha B_\beta} 
    \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{b}^{A_\alpha C_\gamma}\nonumber \\
    &\times
    Y_{cd}^{C_\gamma D_\delta} Y_{ef}^{D_\delta B_\beta}-24
    \sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\alpha}
    \sum_{\gamma=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\delta=1}^{\gamma} 
    g_\alpha g_\beta g_\gamma g_\delta \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{a}^{A_\alpha B_\beta} 
     \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{d}^{C_\gamma D_\delta}\nonumber\\
    &\times
    Y_{ef}^{A_\alpha D_\delta}Y_{bc}^{B_\beta C_\gamma}-\frac{1}{18}\lambda_{abcg}\lambda_{defh}
    \sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}\sum_{\beta=1}^\alpha \mathcal{S}_{\alpha\beta}\left(
    \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{g}^{A_\alpha B_\beta} 
    \left[C_{\phi F^2}\right]_{h}^{A_\alpha B_\beta}\right.
    \nonumber \\&\left.+\left[C_{\phi \tildee F^2}\right]_{g}^{A_\alpha B_\beta} 
    \left[C_{\phi \tildee F^2}\right]_{h}^{A_\alpha
    B_\beta}\right)\Bigg\}\,. 
\end{align}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6\leftarrow \psi^2\phi^3$}{phi6frompsi2phi3}.}

\begin{align}
    \left[\dot{C}_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} &= - \frac{8}{6!}\sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})} \Re{y_{ija}\, y_{jkb}^*\, y_{klc}\left[C_{\psi^2\phi^3}\right]_{lidef}^*}\,.\label{phi6_psi2phi3}
\end{align}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6\leftarrow \psi^2\phi^2\times\psi^2\phi^2$}{phi6frompsi2phi2xpsi2phi2}.}

\begin{align}
    \left[\dot C_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} &= \frac{8}{6!} \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})}\Bigg(\Re{ y^*_{jkc} y^*_{lif} \left[C_{\psi^2\phi^2}\right]_{ijab} \left[C_{\psi^2\phi^2}\right]_{klde}}\\ \nonumber
     &+2 \left[C_{\psi^2\phi^2}\right]_{ijab} y^*_{jkc} \, y_{kld} \left[C_{\psi^2\phi^2}\right]^*_{lief}\Bigg)\,.
     \label{phi6_psi2phi2xpsi2phi2}
\end{align}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$\phi^6\leftarrow \psi^2\phi^2\times\phi^5$}{phi6frompsi2phi2xphi5}.}
\begin{equation}
    \left[\dot{C}_{\phi^6}\right]_{abcdef} = -\frac{1}{36} \sum_{\sigma(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})} \Re{
      y_{ijg} \left[C_{\phi^5}\right]_{abcdg} \left[C_{\psi^2\phi^2}\right]^*_{jief}}
     \label{phi6_psi2phi2xphi5}\,.
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{$D^2\phi^4$}{D2phi4} Class}
\label{sec:D2phi4RGE}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{$D^2\phi^4\leftarrow D^2\phi^4$}{D2phi4fromD2phi4}.}
\label{par:D2phi4<-D2phi4}

The running of the $\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}$ combination defined in Eq.~\eqref{eq:hatCD2phi4} is given by 
\begin{align}
\left[\dot{\widehat C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} &=
-\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G}g_\alpha^2 
\bigg\{
\bigg[
\left(T^\alpha_{cdef}+24T^\alpha_{cedf}
\right)\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{aebf} \nonumber \\
&+ 6\left(T^\alpha_{cedf}+T^\alpha_{cfde}\right)\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abef} - (a\leftrightarrow c) - (b\leftrightarrow d) + 
\binom{a\leftrightarrow c}{b\leftrightarrow d}
\bigg] \nonumber \\
&+ \bigg[
2\left(T^\alpha_{bdef}+24T^\alpha_{bedf}
\right)\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{aecf} + 
\binom{a\leftrightarrow c}{b\leftrightarrow d}
\bigg]
\bigg\}
\nonumber\\
&+ \bigg[
\lambda_{cdef}
\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abef}
- (a\leftrightarrow c) - (b\leftrightarrow d) + 
\binom{a\leftrightarrow c}{b\leftrightarrow d}
\bigg] +\gamma_{c,s}^{ae}\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{ebcd}\nonumber \\
&+\gamma_{c,s}^{be}\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{aecd}+\gamma_{c,s}^{ce}\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abed}+\gamma_{c,s}^{de}\left[\widehat C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abce}\,.\label{eq:D2phi4<-D2phi4}
\end{align}
The running of the full operator (including off-shell contributions) is given by
\begin{align}
\left[\dot{C}_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abcd} &=
\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_G} g_{\alpha}^2 \Big[
-6\Bigl( T^\alpha_{cedf} + T^\alpha_{cfde} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{abef} -6\Bigl( T^\alpha_{aebf} + T^\alpha_{afbe} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{efcd}\nonumber \\
&-\Bigl( T^\alpha_{bdef} + 15\,T^\alpha_{bedf} - 9\,T^\alpha_{bfde} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{aecf} \nonumber\\
&-\Bigl( T^\alpha_{bcef} + 15\,T^\alpha_{becf} - 9\,T^\alpha_{bfce} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{aedf} \nonumber\\
&+\Bigl( T^\alpha_{bdef} + 9\,T^\alpha_{bedf} - 15\,T^\alpha_{bfde} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{afce} \nonumber\\
&+\Bigl( T^\alpha_{bcef} + 9\,T^\alpha_{becf} - 15\,T^\alpha_{bfce} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{afde} \nonumber\\
&-\Bigl( T^\alpha_{adef} + 15\,T^\alpha_{aedf} - 9\,T^\alpha_{afde} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{becf} \nonumber\\
&-\Bigl( T^\alpha_{acef} + 15\,T^\alpha_{aecf} - 9\,T^\alpha_{afce} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{bedf}\nonumber \\
&+\Bigl( T^\alpha_{adef} + 9\,T^\alpha_{aedf} - 15\,T^\alpha_{afde} \Bigr)
\left[C_{D^2\phi^4}\right]_{bfce}\nonumber \\
